to a regular section of P0 near E. Its restriction to E is z α , initially generically and then
everywhere by regularity. Pushing further to the smooth neighborhood of x gives a regular
section with weight initial exactly z α .
    It remains to check global regularity downstairs, since NY was rational and ∆Y could have
negative coefficients. At an original prime B of X away from x, write nB = coeff B (tN ) ∈ Z,
δB = coeff B ∆ ≥ 0, and let gB ≥ 0 be the corresponding coefficient of G. Canonical
coefficients cancel inside the ceiling, and the allowed divisor coefficient of the pushed lifting
bundle is
                                    nB + ⌈−δB − gB /l⌉ ≤ nB .
Thus no forbidden pole appears at any original prime. The point x was already handled by
the local weighted calculation (also when p = 1). Normality now gives a section of OX (tN ).
Its weight initial excludes every monomial preceding α in the original Taylor order, by the
choice of u, and the coefficient of z α is nonzero. Its Taylor initial is therefore α.

Lemma C.6 (Uniform count from the jet body). In the setting of Lemma C.5, K is bounded
and contains the standard simplex Σ = conv(0, e1 , . . . , ep ). For every integer n ≥ 1,

                                  h0 (X, nN ) ≤ (n + p)p vol(K).

Fix any integer t > 1 + 2p. The space spanned by products of n sections of tN has dimension
at least np vol(K).

Proof. On a nonempty open set, the map given by |N | is an immersion and has no base point,
because it is birational in characteristic zero. At the chosen general point its values and
first derivatives thus span all first jets. Suitable linear combinations give initial exponents
0, e1 , . . . , ep , proving Σ ⊂ K. The order of vanishing at this point of a section of jN is at
most Cj: intersect its effective divisor with a general complete intersection curve through
the point, chosen not to lie in its support. Its degree is jN · H p−1 for a fixed sufficiently
ample H, and bounds the local intersection multiplicity. The constant may depend on this
individual example, which suffices to make K bounded.
    For a finite-dimensional space of germs, the number of distinct leading exponents is its
dimension, by successive elimination of leading coefficients; alternatively use the filtration by
the well-ordered monomials, whose nonzero graded pieces are one-dimensional. The exponent
set at degree n is contained in nK ∩ Zp . The disjoint half-open unit boxes based at these
lattice points lie in
                                  nK + [0, 1]p ⊂ nK + pΣ ⊂ (n + p)K.
Their total volume is their number, proving the upper bound.
   For the lower bound let y = nx ∈ nK. Write xi = ai + fi with ai ∈ Z≥0 and 0 ≤ fi < 1.
Choose n integers in coordinate i, namely ai + 1 in exactly ⌊nfi ⌋ of the positions and ai in
the others. Doing this independently in every coordinate gives α(1) , . . . , α(n) with
                           n
                          ∑︂                         (r)
                                α(r) = ⌊y⌋,    0 < αi + 1 − xi < 2.
                          r=1


                                               51
When fi = 0 all entries equal ai , so the strict inequalities still hold. Therefore α(r) + 1 − x ∈
int((t − 1)Σ): all coordinates are positive and their sum is less than 2p < t − 1. Since Σ ⊂ K
and x ∈ K, a neighborhood of α(r) + 1 lies in K + (t − 1)K = tK. Lemma C.5 supplies a
degree-t section with each exponent α(r) . Their product has exponent ⌊y⌋.
    Consequently all lattice points ⌊y⌋, for y ∈ nK, occur among product initials. Their unit
boxes cover nK, so their number is at least vol(nK) = np vol(K). Sections with distinct
initials are linearly independent, proving the claimed lower bound. The case p = 0 is
immediate separately.

C.5    Completion of the saturation count
The field and exact-triviality assertions are already established. We finish by placing both
section counts on one nef integral Weil polarization. The preceding two lemmas then give the
same jet-body volume as the normalization in the upper and lower estimates.

Proof of part (iii) of Lemma C.3. If p = 0, then K∗ = k. By part (i), each fixed-cost section
space is eventually the one-dimensional constant space. Take vi = 1. Assume p > 0 and use
the extraction Wˆ︂ from Lemma C.1 at β1 of (C.38). Let D∗ be the pullback of DW to W       ˆ︂ .
For every rational m > 0, the cutoff spaces identify exactly as function spaces:

                                     S(mPβ1 ) = S(mD∗ ).                                   (C.42)

The negative-MMP error is effective and exceptional, so pushing and pulling the divisor
inequality for an individual rational function identifies the space with the one on the semiample
output. A function in that space belongs to k(W ); the equality Pβ′ 1 = p∗ DW then identifies
its inequalities with those downstairs. Pullback to W  ˆ︂ does not change them. This argument
works for each rational m, with no requirement that mPβ1 be Cartier.
     Every positive coefficient of LD∗ is at least c′ δ, because Lβ1 ≥ c′ and the coefficients of
D∗ /β1 are at least δ. Choose a fixed sufficiently large integer M and put

                                         N = ⌊M LD∗ ⌋.                                     (C.43)

The coefficient lower bound gives N ≥ LD∗ once M is large enough. Increase M so that
N ≥ CD∗ /β1 , with the constant from the field-generation construction. Then S(N ) defines a
birational map and N is big and integral. Run its MMP to a nef and big integral Weil divisor
N ′ on a Q-factorial projective Fano type variety X ′ . For every positive integer j, negativity
gives
                         S(jN ) = S(jN ′ ),    S(jN ′ ) ⊂ S(jM LD).                      (C.44)
Indeed the first equality follows by pushing an effective divisor and, in the reverse direction,
adding the effective exceptional MMP error; the second follows from N ≤ M LD∗ and (C.42).
For every fixed positive integer n, part (i) and the exact plateau identities give eventually

                          S(nLD) ⊂ S(nLD∗ ) ⊂ S(nN ) = S(nN ′ ).                           (C.45)

    Choose an effective ordinary rational klt Calabi–Yau boundary ∆ on X ′ . Such a boundary
is obtained from a klt Fano type boundary by adding a general sufficiently divisible rational
member of its ample anti-log-canonical class. At a general smooth point outside the boundary
and divisor supports, let Ki be the jet body of N ′ in Lemma C.5. Its volume is positive and
finite by Lemma C.6. Set
                                         vi = vol(Ki ).
Equations (C.45) and Lemma C.6 imply

                           dim S(nLD) ≤ (n + p)p vi ≤ (p + 1)p vi np .

                                               52
Fix once and for all an integer t > 1 + 2p. The same lemma supplies a space of products of
n sections of tN ′ of dimension at least vi np . By (C.44), S(tN ′ ) ⊂ S(tM LD). Enlarge the
constant B in part (ii) to at least tM . Those products then lie in the product space in (C.37),
proving the lower bound with the same vi . Both t and M , and hence B, were fixed before n.
This completes the lemma.

Corollary C.7 (Fixed generators and neutral algebraic extensions). In Lemma C.3, let
gi,1 , . . . , gi,ri be any finite list contained in S(C1 Li Di ) for one fixed C1 . For all sufficiently
large i, the entire field Fi = k(gi,1 , . . . , gi,ri ), and its relative algebraic closure inside k(Vi ),
lie in K∗,i . Thus every test from part (ii) is exactly zero on every nonzero element of these
fields at that same index, without a degree-dependent eventual qualification. More generally, a
valuation trivial on a subfield remains trivial on any algebraic extension of it inside its valued
overfield.

Proof. Part (i) of the lemma includes the whole space S(C1 Li Di ) at a single eventual index; it
does not supply separate indices for its individual elements. Field closure gives Fi ⊂ K∗,i . The
cutoff field is relatively algebraically closed, so it contains the stated relative algebraic closure
as well. To see the last assertion directly, let x ̸= 0 be algebraic and choose a polynomial
relation for it with nonzero constant term and coefficients in the trivially valued subfield. If
the value of x were positive, the constant term would have uniquely least value; if it were
negative, the highest nonzero term would have uniquely least value. Neither is possible in a
vanishing sum. Hence its value is zero. This applies to every rational expression and every
polynomial degree once the finite generators have been included, rather than by a further
limit in that degree.


D      Characters and regularization on affine cones
We translate the projective results of Sections B and C into statements about homogeneous
functions on affine cones. The distinction between a primitive prime order and a rescaled
valuation is essential in this translation.

D.1     Angular fields and the discrepancy normalization
Let R̆ be a finitely generated normal domain over k, equipped with a positive torus grading
and a commuting diagonal action of a finite cyclic group G. Positivity means that there is a
linear functional strictly positive on every nonzero occurring weight and that the degree-zero
ring is k. Set R = R̆G and X = Spec R, and assume that X is Q-Gorenstein and klt. We use
the effective torus acting on R, with character lattice M of rank rT > 0. Write

        C = cone{m : Rm ̸= 0} ⊂ MR ,            σ = C ∨ ⊂ Hom(M, R),            K = Frac(R)T .

Thus C is a full-dimensional pointed rational polyhedral cone. Choose a multiplicative
splitting m ↦→ z m of the homogeneous rational functions. Such a splitting exists because M
is free abelian, and it gives
                                    Frac(R) = K(z M ).                                 (D.1)
    Let h ∈ R̆ be a nonzero homogeneous function of G-character χ and torus weight u ∈ MQ .
                                  −1
If rG is the order of G, set H = rG  div(hrG ) on X. Assume that there is a torus-invariant
rational top form θ on X such that

                                 KX (θ) + H = 0,          (X, H) is lc.                            (D.2)

by its exponent. In particular, writing hrG = fh z rG u gives

    Choose a basis z1 , . . . , zrT of the weight monomials. Torus invariance gives a rational top

                                    θ = θ0 ∧ d log z1 ∧ · · · ∧ d log zrT .
Here θ0 is a nonzero constant when K = k. For a primitive P put a(P ) = νlog,P (θ0 ), where
log order is the order of a top form plus one. Extend a homogeneously to positive multiples
of prime orders.

Proposition D.1 (Cone dictionary). In the above setting the following statements hold.
  (i) The discrepancy of (X, H) at (P, y) is a(P ), and its discrepancy at a pure weight is

Proof. Take a uniformizer t at P and separating residue coordinates s1 , . . . , sp−1 , where

follows from lc. Integrality is the ordinary integral log order of θ0 . At a nonzero rational pure

The ray ring is finitely generated and normal: it is the appropriate diagonalizable invariant
subring, followed, if necessary, by a Veronese subring. Its degree-zero part is k, so its Proj is
projective. An invariant rational function is a ratio of two homogeneous elements of the same
weight. Multiplying their common weight by a suitable occurring weight moves both elements
onto a sufficiently divisible multiple of the interior ray. To see the latter assertion directly,
express µ as a strictly positive rational combination of all the finitely many generating weights
and clear denominators; sufficiently high multiples leave room to subtract any prescribed
occurring weight. This proves k(Vµ ) = K.

The polytope on the right is compact because the grading is positive. Its rational vertices
depend on the weights and µ, not on P . After clearing their denominators and taking a
Veronese generated in degree one, these vertices give actual monomials on the ray. Conversely,
a sum of monomials has no worse pole order than its worst summand. Therefore (D.9) is
exactly the maximum allowed pole order in the homogeneous linear system, expressed using
z dµ as rational frame. On the Proj this system is basepoint-free in sufficiently divisible degree.
Its maximum-pole formula on every higher model is the pullback of its ample tautological
divisor. This proves (ii), including the b-divisor assertion. Formula (D.3) identifies D with a
rational principal adjustment of this polarization. Since h is regular upstairs, its invariant
powers have nonnegative value at every Gauss extension, so D ≥ 0.
     For (iii), choose a homogeneous section s on the defining ray whose nonvanishing chart
contains the generic point of P . The map Spec Rs → Spec(Rs )0 is the torus quotient, and
the latter is the corresponding chart of Vµ . Localize the base at P . Its uniformizer remains a
nonunit upstairs: the invariant ring is a direct summand, and an inverse upstairs would give
an inverse downstairs by taking its invariant part. A component of its zero divisor therefore
has height one and dominates P , by the principal ideal theorem. Such a component is torus
invariant, since a connected torus cannot permute its finitely many irreducible components
nontrivially.
    The primitive order of a torus-invariant prime is Gauss. Indeed its valuation ideals are
torus invariant, hence are direct sums of weight spaces; there is no cancellation between
different weights, and on each weight space restriction to K supplies the indicated base order.
Write the resulting valuation as e(P, y). Its restriction to K is an integral multiple eP , and
its values on z M are integral. Invertibility of s implies that y minimizes the
                                                                              (︁
                                                                                 ray polarization.
                                                                                                 )︁
A prime on a normal variety has ordinary log discrepancy one. Thus 1 = e a(P ) + (P, y)(h) ,

                                                 55
which proves (D.7). Notice that this argument concerns actual primes on Vµ ; it asserts no
such identity at an arbitrary exceptional prime.
    On Vµ set Ω = −KVµ (θ0 ). The log discrepancies of (Vµ , Ω) are a, and KVµ (θ0 ) + Ω = 0.
At actual primes (D.7) gives 0 ≤ a ≤ 1; hence Ω is effective. Moreover a(P ) + D(P ) ≤ 1
there. At every divisorial P , a rational minimizer for (D.6) gives a Gauss extension, so klt of
X implies a(P ) + D(P ) > 0. Subtracting the trace of D from Ω and taking D as nef part
gives the claimed generalized klt Calabi–Yau data.
    For completeness these data imply Fano type without any uniform index assumption. On
a common resolution on which D is semiample, replace it by a general divided member of a
sufficiently divisible free system. Added to the generalized boundary this gives an ordinary
klt sub-pair; its pushdown has effective boundary and is crepant, with total log canonical
divisor rationally trivial. The added divisor is big. Choose an effective ample divisor A on Vµ .
Bigness permits a second effective representative of D containing a small positive multiple
of the pullback of A. Mixing this representative with the general free representative with
sufficiently small positive weight preserves klt. The resulting effective boundary downstairs
contains εA for some ε > 0. Subtracting that summand leaves an effective klt boundary
whose negative log canonical class is Q-linearly equivalent to εA, hence ample. Finally, at a
non-floor prime the integer a(P ) > 0 in (D.7) forces (D.8).

D.2    Which divisorial lc tests occur
Lemma D.2 (All divisorial lc tests are Gauss). Every nontrivial divisorial valuation nonneg-
ative on R and having zero discrepancy for (X, H) is Gauss. Its restriction to K is either eP
with a(P ) = 0, or trivial, in which case it is a nonzero pure weight in σ. Conversely every
rational Gauss extension with a(P ) = 0, and every nonzero rational pure weight, has zero
discrepancy and a center whose closure contains the contracting vertex.
    If Spec R̆ → X is quasi-étale and the pairs are related by log pullback, every divisorial
h-lc test upstairs restricts to one of these tests, up to scaling. Conversely all the rational
tests just listed lift to divisorial h-lc tests upstairs whose center closures contain the upstairs
vertex. These statements suffice in particular for testing invariant rational functions.
Proof. Let v be a primitive divisorial valuation as in the first assertion. By the divisorial
restriction statement in Section A, a nontrivial restriction to K has the form eP , with P
primitive and e a positive integer. Use the uniformizer t and separating residue coordinates
from the proof of Proposition D.1. Every remaining logarithmic differential has nonnegative
log order at v. Thus
                                     AX,H (v) ≥ ea(P ).                               (D.10)
More explicitly, pass to the finite-index sublattice of M on which v(z m ) is divisible by
                                                                        m
e, and replace each of its basis monomials by the unit z m t−v(z )/e . If their residues are
algebraically dependent over the residue field of P , their leading differential wedge vanishes, so
the inequality in (D.10) is strict. Zero discrepancy therefore forces a(P ) = 0 and independence
of these angular unit residues. For a finite sum of tied terms, divide by a term of least value.
All differences of its exponents lie in that finite-index lattice; independence of the adjusted
residues prevents cancellation. Thus v is exactly the Gauss valuation determined by its
restrictions to K and z M .
    If the restriction to K is trivial, use a separating transcendence basis of K in the same wedge
computation. If all angular weights were zero, the unit residues could have transcendence
degree at most rT − 1 over K, and the leading top wedge would vanish. Zero log discrepancy
therefore forces a nonzero angular weight. It further forces algebraic independence, over K,
of the residues of a basis of the weight-zero monomials. The same tied-sum argument now
proves that v is a pure Gauss weight. Nonnegativity on R places that weight in σ.

                                                56
    The converse discrepancy assertion was computed in Proposition D.1. The center of a
Gauss valuation is torus invariant. A positive grading contracts its closure to the vertex, so
the vertex lies in that closure; the valuation need not itself be centered at the closed vertex.
    For a finite quasi-étale map with log-pulled pairs, the ramification formula makes discrep-
ancies homogeneous under restriction. Hence an upstairs lc valuation restricts to a downstairs
lc valuation, and every divisorial extension of a downstairs lc valuation is lc upstairs. Such
extensions exist by taking the normalization of a model carrying the downstairs prime. The
finite image of an upstairs center closure is the downstairs center closure and contains the
vertex. There is only one point upstairs over that vertex: every positive homogeneous
semi-invariant generator has an invariant positive power, which vanishes there. Therefore the
upstairs center closure contains the upstairs vertex as claimed.

D.3    Sections and a uniform sandwich argument
Let ξ = f z k ∈ Frac(R) be a nonzero rational eigenfunction, invariant under the finite group,
and let m ∈ Z satisfy µ = k + mu ∈ int C. Define

             Jm (P ) = min (P, y)(ξhm ) = div(f )(P ) + mγ(P ) + L(k + mu)(P ).          (D.11)
                       y∈∆P

This is the b-pullback of an ample rational divisor Jm on Vµ ; the divisor itself may be signed.
At a non-floor prime of Vµ it has integral coefficient, by (D.8) and the integral value of the
finite-group invariant function ξ at a primitive cone prime.
    For a rational b-divisor A that descends to a model, write

                          S(A) = {0} ∪ {g ∈ K∗ : div(g) + A ≥ 0}.

Testing on its determining model is equivalent to testing all divisorial orders. This notation
agrees with that of Section C and also applies to signed divisors.

Lemma D.3 (Sections as regular symbols). For every positive integer q and every g ∈ K,

                              g ∈ S(qJm )    ⇐⇒     gξ q hqm ∈ R̆.                       (D.12)

In particular the neutral functions of cost b ∈ N are precisely

                                 {g ∈ K : ghb ∈ R̆} = S(bD).                             (D.13)

Proof. Regularity implies all Gauss inequalities, hence the implication to the left. Conversely
take a sufficiently divisible power so that the semi-invariant becomes invariant, its weight is
on the integral ray, and its degree is large enough for the ray ring to agree with the section
ring of the tautological polarization in that degree. The divisor inequalities and (D.9) then
put that power in R. The original function belongs to Frac(R̆) and is integral over R̆, so
normality puts it in R̆. The second formula is the case ξ = 1. When K = k, the same
argument says that an interior weight becomes effective in a divisible degree and then uses
normality.

   All statements that follow are sequence statements in bounded dimension. Constants
implicit in O(·) are uniform in the sequence, and hypotheses about small divisorial orders are
imposed along every subsequence, as in Sections A–C.

Proposition D.4 (Sandwich regularization). Let L ≥ 1 vary with the sequence. Choose
integers m > m0 with m − m0 ≥ cL for a fixed c > 0, and suppose both k + m0 u and k + mu



                                              57
are interior. Assume that whenever primitive divisorial valuations P satisfy a(P ) = 0 and
LD(P ) → 0, there are integers jP such that

                           jP ≤ Jm0 (P ) ≤ Jm (P ) ≤ jP + O(LD(P )).                          (D.14)

Then there is a bounded positive integer q and a nonzero g ∈ S(qJm ). Equivalently, gξ q hqm
is regular on Spec R̆.
Proof. For K = k the preceding lemma gives the assertion with q = 1. Otherwise work first
on Vµ , where µ = k + mu. Use the generalized klt Calabi–Yau discrepancy a + D to extract
onto a Q-factorial Fano type model U all exceptional primes below a fixed small positive
discrepancy cutoff, and no other exceptional primes. The extraction statement is supplied
by Section A. All extracted primes are floor primes: a is integral and nonnegative. On U ,
the positive coefficients of the trace DU are precisely the floor primes, and they are at most
one. This holds for surviving primes by (D.7), and for extracted primes by their small a + D
discrepancy.
    For each floor prime Q on U put ρQ = min{1, LD(Q)}. Choose a small fixed rational
c1 > 0, with 2c1 ≤ c, and an effective correction Rc , supported on these primes, such that
                                                      (︁               )︁
                 c1 ρQ ≤ Rc (Q) ≤ 2c1 ρQ ,       den Jm (Q) − Rc (Q) ≤ C1 /ρQ .               (D.15)

Here den is the reduced positive denominator. Such a choice is obtained by taking a
rational grid of mesh at most c1 ρQ and denominator at most C1 /ρQ in the interval [Jm (Q) −
2c1 ρQ , Jm (Q) − c1 ρQ ]. There is no correction outside the floor: no such primes were extracted,
and Jm is integral there.
    Minima of sums give the b-divisor inequality

                                    Jm − Jm0 ≥ (m − m0 )D.                                    (D.16)

Since Rc ≤ 2c1 LDU , the divisor PU = (Jm )U − Rc dominates the trace of Jm0 and is big.
Run its MMP on U to a nef big semiample divisor P ′ on a Q-factorial Fano type model U ′ .
On a common higher model, denote pullback from a displayed model by a star. Then

                    Jm ≥ (P ′ )∗ ≥ Jm0 ,      Rc∗ ≤ E := Jm − (P ′ )∗ ≤ (Rc′ )∗ .             (D.17)

Here is justification for both sides, including the comparison with the possibly different ray m0 .
The divisor Jm descends on U , and MMP negativity gives (P ′ )∗ ≤ PU∗ = Jm − Rc∗ . For the
middle inequality, every divisible section of Jm0 is a section of PU , hence of P ′ by the MMP.
The maximum-pole description of semiample divisors, applied on a common determination,
gives (P ′ )∗ ≥ Jm0 . Finally b-nef negativity gives Jm ≤ (Jm′ )∗ ; since J ′ = P ′ + R′ , this is the
                                                                           m           c
last inequality in (D.17). Also ρQ ≥ DU (Q), so

                                    E ≥ Rc∗ ≥ c1 (DU )∗ ≥ c1 D.

The final comparison is again b-nef negativity for D.
    We apply Lemma B.1 to the polarization P ′ . Choose fixed rational δ2 > 0 small enough
for the denominator bound below, and then 0 < δ1 ≤ δ2 c1 . For 0 ≤ t ≤ δ2 use the discrepancy
family and nef part

                                   at = a + (1 − t/δ2 )δ1 D + tE,
                                  Mt = (1 − t/δ2 )δ1 D + tJm .

Their total log canonical divisor is Q-linearly equivalent to tP ′ . For t ≥ δ2 keep at = a + δ2 E
and add (t − δ2 )(P ′ )∗ to the nef part. This gives families on arbitrarily long parameter
intervals. They are nondecreasing, klt, and have nef Q-Cartier b-parts.

                                                 58
    We check effectivity and the denominator trace requirement of that lemma. Every prime
of U ′ survives from U . At a non-floor prime, a = 1, D = E = 0, and P ′ has integral coefficient.
At a surviving floor prime Q, E(Q) = Rc (Q) ≤ 2c1 ρQ , while

                                            1        ρQ
                                              ′
                                                   ≥    .
                                        den(P (Q))   C1

Take 2δ2 c1 C1 ≤ 1. Monotonicity gives 0 < at (Q) ≤ δ2 Rc (Q) throughout the family, so

    It remains to check the signed phase hypothesis. At a growing parameter, small discrep-
ancies mean a(P ) = 0 and E(P ) → 0. Because E ≥ c1 D, these valuations eventually belong
to the fixed-cutoff list already extracted on U . On that list, (D.15) and (D.17) give

                                   E(P ) ≥ c1 min{1, LD(P )}.

Thus LD(P ) → 0 and LD(P ) = O(E(P )). Combining (D.14) with (D.17) yields

                                jP ≤ (P ′ )∗ (P ) ≤ jP + O(E(P )).

The phase of (P ′ )∗ (P ) therefore has a nonnegative lift of size O(at (P )), which is stronger than
the signed phase hypothesis. Lemma B.1 supplies a nonzero section in a bounded positive
degree of P ′ . By (D.17) it lies in S(qJm ), and Lemma D.3 finishes the proof.

D.4     Bounded multiples, character counts, and tilted heights
The sandwich proposition turns a phase interval into a regular symbol. We use it in three
forms: to supply symbols for bounded-width lattice characters, to count symbols with one
character and weight, and to supply a symbol whose cost reflects a small tilted height. The
last form will be used on newly optimized cones before any further rank refinement.

Corollary D.5 (Bounded-multiple character regularization). Suppose that the order condition
(C.5) on Vu , with error ηi → 0, holds whenever a(P ) = 0 and LD(P ) → 0. Fix C0 > 0. If
k ∈ M satisfies
                              |⟨y, k⟩| ≤ C0 L⟨y, u⟩   (y ∈ σ),                       (D.18)
there are a bounded positive integer q, a nonzero invariant rational eigenfunction Y = f z qk ,
and a positive integer b ≤ C2 L such that hb Y ∈ R̆. The constants are uniform in k. Both
signs of k may be treated with the same bounded multiplier, and their costs may be increased
to a common cost O(L).
    The same bounds hold uniformly if L is replaced by any L′ ≥ L and (D.18) is imposed at
scale L′ .

Proof. Choose an integer m1 ≍ L with m1 > C0 L, so that both ±k + m1 u lie in int C.
Bigness of their ray polarizations supplies rational-power functions g+ , g− on K such that

                    (P, y)(z ±k hm1 ) + P (g± ) ≥ 0   for every P and y ∈ ∆P .

Here a rational-power function means a positive integral root of an element of K∗ used only
through its Q-principal order; it need not be an element of K. Put

                          ℓ = − div(g+ ),      G = 2m1 D + div(g+ g− ).




                                                 59
Adding the two Gauss inequalities and taking the height minimum shows that G ≥ 0 on Vu ,
and G ∼Q 2m1 D. At a non-floor prime Q of this model, choose the integral minimizing y in
(D.8). Since its height is zero, the two inequalities give

                          0 ≤ ⟨y, k⟩ − ℓ(Q) ≤ G(Q),         ⟨y, k⟩ ∈ Z.

Lemma C.2 therefore gives a fixed positive integer q0 and a fixed A > 0 such that, on all tiny
sequences in the statement, one can choose integers jP with

                                  |q0 ℓ(P ) − jP | ≤ ALD(P ).

  Apply Proposition D.4 to ξ = z q0 k . For any sufficiently large integer m0 = O(L) and
m = 2m0 , the first Gauss inequality gives

                                 Jm0 ≥ q0 ℓ + (m0 − q0 m1 )D.

At a height-minimizing angular vector the second inequality gives

               Jm ≤ q0 ℓ + (m − q0 m1 )D + q0 G ≤ q0 ℓ + (m + q0 m1 )D + q0 G.

The order condition (C.5), applied to G/(2m1 ) ∼Q D, bounds G(P ) ≤ 2m1 (1 + ηi )D(P ) on
the tiny sequences. Choose m0 −q0 m1 ≥ AL. These estimates give exactly (D.14). A bounded
further multiplier q1 then supplies gz q1 q0 k hq1 m ∈ R̆. This is the desired regularization.
    Apply the argument to −k as well, raise the two resulting functions to bounded powers
to align their multipliers, and multiply by powers of h to align their costs. Uniformity in the
weight follows by applying the sequence argument to any proposed sequence of exceptions.
For an enlarged scale L′ ≥ L, its tiny sequences satisfy LD(P ) → 0 as well. All the preceding
hypotheses therefore hold with L′ in place of L. A sequence of exceptional pairs (L′ , k) would
contradict the same proof; this proves the stated uniformity, including for growing L′ /L.

E     Optimization on common log canonical slices
Throughout this section, x ∈ S is a fixed normal complex affine germ of positive dimension
n, S is Q-Gorenstein and klt, and R = OS,x , m = mx , and A = AS . A valuation over x is
nonnegative on R; its centre may be a proper generization of x. A valuation centred at x has
v(m) > 0. Unless otherwise stated, valuations are nontrivial quasi-monomial real valuations.
Discrepancy is extended linearly in the weights on a log-smooth presentation. For every
valuation v over x, put v(0) = +∞ and, for t ≥ 0, set

                at (v) = {f ∈ R : v(f ) ≥ t},              a>t (v) = {f ∈ R : v(f ) > t}.

The associated graded algebra and initial form are
                             ⨁︂
                   grv R =         at (v)/a>t (v),         inv (f ) = [f ]v(f )   (f ̸= 0).
                             t≥0

Degrees outside the value semigroup have zero summand. These conventions also apply when
the centre is a proper generization of x. For a valuation centred at x, set
                                                     n! length(R/at (v))
                               vol(v) = lim                              .
                                           t−→∞              tn
The limit exists by Cutkosky’s theorem for graded families of m-primary ideals [11, Theorem 1.1
(=5.5)]. Its hypothesis on the nilradical of the completion holds because R is excellent and
normal, hence has reduced completion. The theorem applies first to integral thresholds;
squeezing between adjacent integral thresholds gives the displayed real-parameter limit. For
a klt pair (S, ∆) and a centred valuation of finite log discrepancy, the normalized volume is
                                                           n
                                     vol S,∆ (v) = AS,∆ (v) vol(v).
                                     ˆ︂

Its nth root is AS,∆ (v) vol(v)1/n , with the same minimizers. Here and throughout the volume
formulas, n = dim S.
    Fix a nonzero h ∈ R such that (S, H) is lc, where H = div(h), and finitely many nonzero
Fj ∈ R and mj ∈ Z>0 . The common lc slice is

                    Q = {v : A(v) = v(h) = 1,              v(Fj ) ≥ mj for every j}.                (E.1)

Assume that it contains a centred valuation. Its cone of rays is defined by A(v) = v(h) > 0
and v(Fj ) ≥ mj v(h). An allowed height is
                               ∑︂
            b(v) = b0 v(h) −        bℓ v(Gℓ ),       bℓ ≥ 0,      Gℓ ∈ R \ {0},          b|Q > 0.   (E.2)
                               ℓ

On centred valuations for which b(v) > 0, the degree-one homogeneous height b similarly
gives the scale-invariant quantity b(v)n vol(v) and its nth root b(v) vol(v)1/n , with the same

                                                      62
minimizers. All constants in this section may depend on this individual germ and its finite
data. The constants in the strict volume gap below depend only on dimension and the stated
separation.
    Our goal is to minimize this functional on the common lc rays and, for rational heights,
realize its minimizer as an ordinary normalized-volume minimizer of an effective rational klt
pair. The realization must be exact: finite generation will be applied to that pair and its
actual minimizer. We first control retraction to the lc locus and volume under interpolation;
these estimates will allow one rational perturbation to exclude every competing valuation
outside the common slice.

E.1    Retractions, jets, and the topology of the slice
Let P → S be a smooth model, F a reduced snc divisor on P , and v a valuation with centre
on P . Write rF (v) for its monomial retraction using the components of F through the centre,
with their v-weights. It is the trivial valuation if there are no such components. Retraction
decreases the value of every function regular on the chart and preserves the values of its
boundary monomials; see [17, Lemma 4.7, Corollary 4.8, and Proposition 5.1]. The following
quantitative strengthening is needed here.

Lemma E.1 (Logarithmic jet estimate). Fix finitely many regular affine functions on S. On
a fixed resolution P , with F reduced snc, there is a constant C such that, for every polynomial
g ̸= 0 of degree at most D in those functions and every quasi-monomial v over x,

                        rF (v)(g) ≤ v(g) ≤ rF (v)(g) + C(D + 1)AP,F (v).                       (E.3)

The same constant works when the centre is not closed and when some weights disappear on
passing to a face of the monomial cone.

Proof. Cover the relevant part of P by finitely many smooth etale coordinate charts. At the
centre of v, write the coordinates of the components of F through that centre as z1 , . . . , zs and
the remaining coordinates as y1 , . . . , yn−s . Thus v(zi ) > 0. Choose a closed specialization of
the centre in this chart, avoiding the other components of F , and translate the y-coordinates
to vanish there. Both v and its retraction are nonnegative on this closed-point local ring: the
original centre generalizes this point. A unit in that ring consequently has value zero for both
valuations. Put ai = v(zi ) and expand g in the completed local ring. The least z-weight is

                           d0 = min{⟨a, α⟩ : [z α y β ]g ̸= 0 for some β}.

We first prove a degree bound independent of a.
   The function field of the chart is a finite extension of C(z, y). In a fixed basis, multiplication
by each of the finitely many affine functions has a matrix with fixed rational-function entries.
A polynomial of degree at most D in these matrices has a common denominator of degree
O(D + 1) and numerator entries of the same degree. Its characteristic equation, after clearing
denominators, gives
                            q
                           ∑︂
                                 pj (z, y)g j = 0,        deg pj ≤ C0 (D + 1),                 (E.4)
                           j=0

where q is fixed and the coefficients are not all zero. Translations of coordinates do not change
this degree bound. Give all coordinates strictly positive rationally independent weights. Let
γ be the leading exponent of g, and let δj be the leading exponent of a nonzero pj . At least
two distinct powers, say j and k, have the least weight in (E.4). Independence of the weights
gives
                                        δj + jγ = δk + kγ.

                                                     63
Consequently |γ| ≤ C1 (D + 1), after enlarging the constant. This conclusion holds for every
such choice of positive weights.
    Now give the z-coordinates their fixed positive weights and give the y-coordinates weight
zero. There are finitely many z-exponents of weight d0 , and only finitely many in a sufficiently
small fixed neighbourhood of this weight. Every vertex of the convex hull of the tied z-
exponents is exposed by a small generic perturbation of the z-weights. Give the transverse
coordinates sufficiently small positive generic weights afterwards. The leading exponent
projects to that vertex: the positive gap to the other z-weights prevents an exponent outside
the least face from entering. The preceding bound therefore bounds every vertex, and
convexity bounds every tied z-exponent by C1 (D + 1). The same perturbation supplies at
least one pair (α, β) on the least face with |α| + |β| ≤ C1 (D + 1).
    For this choice of α, apply the product, over the z-coordinates, of the operators
                                             ∏︂
                                                       (zi ∂zi − l).
                                        0≤l≤C1 (D+1)
                                            l̸=αi

It kills every other tied z-exponent and multiplies the chosen coefficient by a nonzero constant.
Apply a transverse derivative ∂yβ for a bounded exponent occurring in that coefficient. Its
new constant term is nonzero. These operators have total order O(D + 1), and do not change
any z-exponent. Their result g ′ satisfies

                          g ′ = uz α     (mod I>d0 ),         u a local unit,               (E.5)

where I>d0 is the monomial ideal generated by the z-monomials of weight greater than d0 .
This ideal is finitely generated. The assertion descends from completion by faithful flatness.
More explicitly, faithful flatness first gives g ′ = uz α + q with u regular and q ∈ I>d0 . In the
completion, u differs from the formal unit coefficient by an element of (I>d0 : z α ). This colon
ideal is proper, because z α has weight d0 , and hence lies in the local maximal ideal. Thus u
is a unit. Each monomial generator of I>d0 has value greater than d0 for both v and rF (v),
and its regular coefficients have nonnegative value. Thus

                              v(g ′ ) = rF (v)(g ′ ) = d0 = rF (v)(g).

This also explains why a nonclosed centre causes no problem.
   For completeness, a regular logarithmic vector field L satisfies

                                       v(Lf ) ≥ v(f ) − AP,F (v).                           (E.6)

For a prime-order valuation, take a local logarithmic volume form η for (P, F ) and use

                                   (Lf )η = f d log(f ) ∧ ιL η.

At the prime on a higher smooth model, logarithmic coordinate forms and d log(f ) have
nonnegative logarithmic order. Comparing the two sides gives (E.6). Homogeneity gives it for
rational monomial weights, and rational approximation gives it for arbitrary quasi-monomial
weights. In this approximation the signs on a fixed chart ring can be retained: the values of its
finitely many generators are rational piecewise linear functions on the monomial weight cone,
so one approximates in their common sign cells. Discrepancy and the finitely many function
values in question are continuous there. The Euler operators and transverse derivatives used
above are logarithmic vector fields, and subtracting a scalar does not worsen the estimate.
Iterating (E.6) and using (E.5) proves the upper bound in (E.3). The lower bound is the
retraction inequality.

                                                  64
    Suppose more generally that Ψ is an effective lc rational sum of Cartier divisors. Resolve
its support and any further finite list of ideals and divisors, and write

          KP + BP = π ∗ (KS + Ψ),          F = BP=1 ,          e = AS,Ψ (v),    r = rF (v).

If E is the reduced union of the support of BP , the exceptional locus, and the additional
resolved divisors, then
                                             ∑︂
                           e = AP,E (v) +         (1 − coeff D BP )v(D).
                                            D⊂E

All summands are nonnegative. The positive numbers 1 − coeff D BP for D ̸⊂ F have a
positive minimum on this fixed resolution. Therefore

                          AP,F (v) ≤ Ce,          |AS (v) − AS (r)| ≤ Ce,                     (E.7)

and the evaluations of each resolved ideal or divisor differ at v and r by at most Ce.
These statements follow also when BP has negative coefficients: the displayed coefficients
1 − coeff D BP are still positive away from F . In particular, if e = 0, then Lemma E.1 gives
equality on every polynomial in the fixed affine generators. Equality extends to the local ring
and its fraction field by localization. Hence v = r.

Lemma E.2 (Compactness and uniform centring). The slice Q is a compact finite polyhedral
complex of monomial valuations on any log resolution adapted to h and the Fj . Evaluations of
fixed functions are continuous on it. For centred valuations with A(v) = 1, there are constants
c, C > 0 depending on the germ such that
                                                                  c
                                 vol(v) ≥ c,        vol(v) ≥         .                        (E.8)
                                                                v(m)

Volume is continuous on the centred part of Q.

Proof. Apply (E.7) to Ψ = H. Every zero-discrepancy valuation is monomial on the coefficient-
one part of the fixed resolution. Conversely those monomial valuations have zero discrepancy
for (S, H). Retain the strata whose closed images contain x. This condition is preserved
when a stratum is replaced by a larger stratum, so the retained cones include their faces. On
                                                          ∑︁
a cone with primitive generators Ei the equation A(v) = i ai A(Ei ) = 1 cuts out a compact
simplex, because A(Ei ) > 0. The additional inequalities are linear on an adapted resolution.
There are finitely many cones. Function evaluations are monomial minima on them and agree
on faces by localization to the larger stratum, proving compactness and continuity.
    We use Li’s Izumi estimate [24, Theorem 3.1]:

                      v(f ) ≤ CI A(v) ordm (f )    (f ∈ R \ {0}, v centred).                  (E.9)

Thus, when A(v) = 1, the valuation ideal at (v) is contained in m⌈t/CI ⌉ . Hilbert–Samuel
growth gives the first bound in (E.8). Choose fixed generators z1 , . . . , zq of m, and let z = zi
have v(z) = δ = v(m). Since R is a domain,

                                     (at (v) : z j ) = at−jδ (v).

Filtering R/at (v) by the images of z j R gives
                                            ∑︂            (︁                   )︁
                    length(R/at (v)) =             length R/(at−jδ (v) + (z)) .
                                         0≤j<t/δ



                                                  65
For j ≤ t/(2δ), Izumi bounds this summand below by length(R/(m⌈t/(2CI )⌉ + (z))). The latter
grows as a positive constant times tn−1 ; this includes n = 1, when it is a positive constant.
Since z belongs to a fixed finite list, the lower constant can be chosen uniformly. Divide by
tn /n! to obtain the second bound in (E.8).
     To prove continuity, consider valuations converging on one monomial cone to a centred
limit. Drop the components whose weights tend to zero, and call the resulting retractions
ri . The retained positive weights converge multiplicatively. On regular functions, the ri are
therefore multiplicatively 1 + o(1) equivalent to the limit. Their maximal-ideal values, and
those of the original valuations, are bounded below. The reduced-log discrepancy measuring
the dropped weights tends to zero. Lemma E.1 upgrades this fact to a multiplicative
comparison on all regular functions as follows.
     Write q = ordm (f ) ≥ 1. With c0 > 0 a common lower bound for maximal-ideal values and
C0 q the common Izumi upper bound for the values of f , fix L with Lc0 > 2C0 . Represent
f modulo mN , N = ⌈Lq⌉, by a polynomial p of degree at most N in fixed generators of
m. Such a representative exists also for localized regular functions by expanding their unit
denominators modulo mN . The error has value greater than both values of f , so vi (p) = vi (f )
and ri (p) = ri (f ). The jet estimate bounds their difference by o(1)q, uniformly in f . Since
ri (f ) ≥ c0 q, it follows that ri ≤ vi ≤ (1 + o(1))ri on all regular functions. Units have value
zero. Inclusion of valuation ideals now proves volume continuity. Every convergent sequence
has a subsequence in one of the finitely many cones, which proves the assertion on the whole
centred part.

   We record explicitly the uniform version of the truncation argument.
Corollary E.3 (Simultaneous approximation). In the situation of (E.7), suppose AS (v) and
AS (r) are bounded above and v(m), r(m) ≥ c0 > 0. Then a single constant C works for every
such v and every f ∈ R:
                               r(f ) ≤ v(f ) ≤ (1 + Ce)r(f ).                       (E.10)
In particular, vol(v)1/n ≥ (1 + Ce)−1 vol(r)1/n .
Proof. Use N = ⌈L ordm (f )⌉ with the single constant L chosen in the preceding proof.
Equations (E.3) and (E.7) give v(f ) − r(f ) ≤ Ce ordm (f ) ≤ Ce r(f )/c0 . Enlarge C. The
ideal inclusions give the volume inequality.

E.2    Convex domination and strict volume comparison

The failure of klt is witnessed by a prime E whose centre contains x, and A(E) ≤

whereas lc of (S, H) gives u(h) ≤ A(u) = 1. It is already in Q.
    Here is the geometric mechanism behind (E.12). Resolve the summand ideals and their
sum, with fixed divisors Di , D, and subdivide {pi ≥ 0 :         pi = 1/N } by the rounding
                                                              ∑︁

walls of OP (⌈KP/S − i pi Di ⌉). The cellular complex indexed by chains of nonempty cells,
                         ∑︁

with the usual alternating face maps and inclusions on removing the first cell, resolves
OP (⌈KP/S − D/N ⌉). In completed snc coordinates its exactness is coefficientwise: the cells
admitting a given Laurent monomial form a convex relatively open subset of the exponent
simplex, and their barycentric order complex is contractible when nonempty. This subset is
nonempty exactly for monomials in the target. Indeed, locally one summand divided by the
invertible sum ideal is a unit, so its fixed divisor there equals D. Local vanishing for each
mixed multiplier-ideal term preserves the surjective augmentation under pushforward. This
gives the stated sum formula in precisely the singular setting used above.
    Let the rational lower targets increase to their averaged values. Compactness from
Lemma E.2 gives a point meeting the finite list exactly as lower bounds. The closed sets
imposing these lower bounds, indexed by all regular functions, have the finite intersection
property. Compactness supplies a single u satisfying (E.11) for every function. Positivity on
                                                                                    ∑︁
generators of m proves the centring assertion. For unnormalized inputs, put a = s ps A(vs ),
apply the normalized assertion with weights ps A(vs )/a to vs /A(vs ), and multiply the result
by a. The formula for b and the equality for u(h) prove its height bound.

Corollary E.5 (Opposite ratios). Suppose rational functions q, q ′ have regular numerators
and power-of-h denominators, and v(q ′ ) = −v(q) on Q. The interpolant in Lemma E.4
has u(q) = s ps vs (q) and u(q ′ ) = s ps vs (q ′ ). The assertion holds simultaneously for any
           ∑︁                       ∑︁

number of such pairs.

Proof. Apply domination to both numerators and use the exact value of h. The two lower
inequalities have opposite sides and so must be equalities.

A sufficiently small fixed relative error similarly gives any prescribed fixed multiplicative

                                                   68
E.3    Exact realization by a rational klt pair
We have established compactness, a uniform comparison with monomial retraction, and strict
volume improvement when two valuations differ. The remaining task is to encode the finite
order constraints in an lc boundary and then choose one rational perturbation for which
every ordinary minimizer lies exactly on its zero-discrepancy locus. The parameter must
work for all competing valuations at once.

Lemma E.7 (A boundary encoding the common slice). Choose positive rational numbers θj
with j θj < 1. For sufficiently general scalars aj , put Ej = div(hmj + aj Fj ) and
    ∑︁

                                               ∑︂             ∑︂ θj
                                  Ψ = (1 −          θj )H +                 Ej .         (E.16)
                                               j                   j
                                                                       mj

Then Ψ is effective and lc, and its nonzero zero-discrepancy rays over the germ are exactly
the common lc rays. On every such ray,

                                     v(hmj + aj Fj ) = mj v(h).                          (E.17)

In particular, the coefficients θj may be chosen arbitrarily small while retaining these conclu-
sions.

Proof. Set bj = (hmj , Fj ). For any valuation,
                             ∑︂                ∑︂ θj
               A(v) − (1 −        θj )v(h) −              v(bj )
                             j                  j

                                        ∑︂

                                          j

Equality holds exactly on the common lc rays, together with the trivial valuation. Resolve
the ideals and choose the Ej as general members of their pencils. On the resolution the
residual free members meet the existing boundary with snc support and contain none of
its strata. Their coefficients θj /mj are less than one. They therefore introduce no new
zero-discrepancy strata, proving the assertion about Ψ. On the coefficient-one monomial
skeleton, each free residual member has value zero because it contains no stratum. The value
of its pencil member is consequently the fixed-ideal value min{mj v(h), v(Fj )} = mj v(h),
proving (E.17).

Theorem E.8 (Exact common-slice minimizer). For every allowed height b, the homogeneous
functional b(v) vol(v)1/n on centred common lc rays has a unique minimizing ray. Its A = 1
representative depends continuously on the coefficients of b while the height stays strictly
positive on the fixed slice. It is fixed by every automorphism preserving the slice and the
height.
    If the coefficients of b are rational, its minimizer w is the normalized-volume minimizer
of an effective rational klt pair on x ∈ S. More precisely, one can choose an effective rational
lc Cartier boundary Ψ with exactly the common lc rays as its zero-discrepancy locus, and one
positive rational number λ, fixed independently of the competing valuation, such that
                                                            ∑︂

is effective and klt, and w minimizes vol
                                      ˆ︂ S,Ψ among all centred valuations. On the common

                                                     69
    For the same rational height, the algebra grw R is finitely generated and normal. Its
spectrum S 0 is a klt Q-Gorenstein cone. Write h0 , Fj0 , G0ℓ for initial forms, w0 for the grading
valuation, and Q0 , b0 for the analogous central slice and height. Then (S 0 , div(h0 )) is lc,
AS 0 (w0 ) = w0 (h0 ) = 1, w0 ∈ Q0 , and b0 is strictly positive on Q0 . The valuation w0

Proof. Attainment, uniqueness, and continuity. Compactness gives minQ b > 0. A minimizing
sequence has bounded volume, so (E.8) bounds its maximal-ideal values away from zero. A
subsequence converges in Q to a centred valuation. Lemma E.2 gives volume continuity and
hence attainment. If two minimizing rays existed, normalize each by b = 1. Their midpoint
has a dominating common lc interpolant u with b(u) ≤ 1. If the minimum value of b vol1/n
is M , then vol(u) ≥ M n /b(u)n ≥ M n . The strict gap (E.14) contradicts this unless the two
height-normalized valuations agree on every regular function. Thus they are the same ray.
For heights whose coefficients converge to those of a positive height, the minimum heights stay
bounded below and one fixed centred competitor bounds the minimum values above. The
same compactness argument shows that every subsequential limit of minimizers is the unique
limiting minimizer. This proves continuity. Automorphisms preserving the data preserve the
functional, so uniqueness proves equivariance.
    Perturbing the boundary. Assume from now on that the height is rational. Choose Ψ
by Lemma E.7, and resolve additionally all Gℓ and m. For small rational λ > 0, (E.18) is
effective, since the coefficient of H in (E.16) is positive. It is klt near x: on each coefficient-one
component its new discrepancy is λb > 0, while the other finitely many resolved discrepancies
remain positive for sufficiently small λ. The log-smooth criterion then tests every valuation.
    Uniform control of every relevant competitor. Let v be a centred quasi-monomial valuation
with A(v) = 1 and
                                     AS,Ψλ (v) vol(v)1/n ≤ λM0 ,                                (E.19)

are uniform over all valuations in (E.19) and all sufficiently small λ.

obtaining r. Izumi bounds every fixed function value at v when A(v) = 1; thus |b(v)| ≤ C.
The first bound in (E.8) and (E.19) give

                                  e + λb(v) ≤ Cλ,          e ≤ Cλ.

                           AS (r) = 1 + O(e),          b(v) = b(r) + O(e).                    (E.20)

                                  β ≤ b(r) ≤ B,         b(v) ≥ β/2.

The sublevel inequality now bounds vol(v) above. The second bound in (E.8) bounds v(m)

                            v(f ) ≤ (1 + Ce)r(f )        for every f ∈ R,                     (E.21)

Choose one positive rational λ small enough for all preceding bounds and for λ(C ′ + CB) < 1.

merely a limiting assertion as λ → 0. The argument applies for every smaller positive rational
parameter satisfying the same bounds.
     Stable degeneration of this actual minimizer. We now apply stable degeneration to the
effective rational klt pair (S, Ψλ ) and its actual minimizer w. By [38, Theorems 1.1–1.2], its
associated graded is finitely generated and defines a normal klt log Fano cone pair (S 0 , Ψ0λ ; w0 ).
The grading minimizes normalized volume on the central pair; ordinary volume and pair
discrepancy are preserved, as in [25, Lemma 4.10 and Theorem 3.5, arXiv version]. The
general uniqueness theorem for the klt-pair minimizer is [37, Theorem 1.1]; the constrained
uniqueness needed here was proved before invoking it.
     Each component used in (E.18) is the divisor of a regular function. For a principal ideal
(f ) and a valuation filtration, multiplicativity gives inw ((f )) = (inw f ). Equivalently its Rees
homogenization, using its exact order, cuts a Cartier divisor without a fibre component. Hence
Ψ0λ is an effective rational sum of principal Cartier divisors. Since KS 0 + Ψ0λ is Q-Cartier,
so is KS 0 , and removing the effective boundary shows that S 0 is klt. Preservation of pair
discrepancy and of all equation orders gives
                AS 0 (w0 ) = AS (w) = 1,        w0 (h0 ) = 1,     w0 (Fj0 ) = w(Fj ) ≥ mj .
    The choice of the θj can be made arbitrarily small. For each such choice the exact
minimizer is still the unique w, and thus the graded algebra remains the same. Choose
the corresponding λ smaller as necessary. The central effective klt boundaries then contain
(1 − o(1)) div(h0 ). For every prime over S 0 , the resulting discrepancy inequalities imply, on
taking the coefficient limit, AS 0 (E) ≥ ordE (h0 ). This proves that (S 0 , div(h0 )) is lc.
    Finally take v ′ ∈ Q0 . By (E.17), the initial equation of hmj + aj Fj is either (h0 )mj , or

Corollary E.9 (Near-minimizers for a height). Fix an allowed height b and normalize its
common rays by b = 1. If wi , vi have functional values within relative o(1) of the minimum,
then wi = (1 + o(1))vi on all regular functions, with an error independent of the function. The
same conclusion, after A = 1 normalization, holds for minimizers of heights whose coefficients
converge to those of b.

                                                        71
Proof. For the height-normalized pair, the midpoint interpolant has height at most one. Its
volume is therefore at least the minimum of volume on the height-one slice. The proof using
(E.14) applies verbatim: any fixed separation on one function would force a fixed relative
volume loss. For converging heights bi , positivity on the compact slice gives bi /b = 1 + o(1)
uniformly. Their minimizing rays, normalized by b = 1, are thus near-minimizers for b, and
the preceding assertion applies. Evaluation on h, which equals A on the common rays, shows
that the factors needed to normalize by A = 1 converge as well.

Corollary E.10 (Domination by the minimizer). For the minimizing ray w and every v ∈ Q,

                                 v(f )      w(f )
                                       ≤ 2n              (f ∈ R).                           (E.24)
                                 b(v)       b(w)

For rational heights the same statement holds on the central slice with all symbols carrying a
superscript 0.

Proof. Normalize both rays by b = 1 and interpolate them with weights 1/2. The resulting
centred u has b(u) ≤ 1, so minimality gives vol(u) ≥ vol(w). For w(f ) > 0, (E.13) gives

                                                 w(f )
                                     1 ≤ 2n                 .
                                              w(f ) + v(f )

Thus v(f ) ≤ (2n − 1)w(f ), which implies the stated bound. If f is a unit both values are
zero. The central assertion follows from the same argument and Theorem E.8.

Lemma E.11 (One-sided height tilt). Let G ∈ R \ {0}, m ∈ Z>0 , and g(v) = v(G)/m − v(h).
Suppose g(v)/v(h) ≥ 2c on some common lc ray, where c > 0 is rational. Set

        M = max g ≥ 2c,        µ∗ = (M − c)−1 ,          bµ (v) = v(h) + µ(cv(h) − g(v)).
               Q

For 0 ≤ µ < µ∗ the height is positive. There is a rational µ in this interval whose A = 1
minimizing valuation w satisfies

                                  g(w) > 0,          bµ (w) ≥ 1.

Moreover bµ ≤ 2 + 1/c on Q, and the corresponding central height obeys the same bound on
Q0 .

Proof. As µ ↑ µ∗ , the minimum of bµ vol1/n tends to zero. To prove this even when an
endpoint zero occurs at a noncentred valuation v, interpolate v with one fixed centred point
w1 ∈ Q, giving w1 weight t. For 0 < µ∗ − µ ≤ t, the interpolant u has bµ (u) = O(t).
Since v(h) = w1 (h) = 1, the improved volume estimate (E.13), applied with f = h, gives
vol(u) ≤ t1−n vol(w1 ). Hence bµ (u) vol(u)1/n = O(t1/n ). The lower volume bound in (E.8)
then forces the minimizing height to tend to zero. In particular its minimizing valuation
eventually has g(w) > c.
    If the minimizer at µ = 0 has g(w) > 0, take µ = 0. Otherwise continuous dependence in
Theorem E.8 gives a nonempty open interval of parameters for which 0 < g(w) < c. Choose a
rational parameter in that interval. Then bµ (w) = 1 + µ(c − g(w)) ≥ 1. Finally v(G) ≥ 0 gives
g(v) ≥ −1 on the normalized slice, while µ < µ∗ ≤ 1/c. Thus bµ (v) ≤ 1 + (c + 1)/c = 2 + 1/c.
The same calculation applies centrally because G0 is regular and v ′ (h0 ) = 1 on Q0 .




                                                72
F     Degeneration, exact sign transfer, and rank refinement
We work with a complex klt germ x ∈ S of dimension n, a nonzero regular function h such
that (S, H) is lc, where H = div(h), and a rational top form ω with divS (ω) = 0. Thus the
reflexive canonical sheaf is trivialized by ω. A finite cyclic group G fixes x and acts quasi-étale
on S; we replace it by its effective image. Suppose that h and ω have the same character χ.
In particular, ω/h descends to the quotient. Fix invariant ratios

                        fj = Fj /hmj ,        Fj ∈ OS,x \ {0},        mj ∈ N>0 .

The common slice consists of the h-lc valuations satisfying q(fj ) ≥ 0, with the convention on
centres from Section E: the closed image of the centre contains x. Unless another normalization
is displayed, q(h) = AS (q) = 1.
    Let w be a centred G-invariant quasi-monomial valuation for which

                                             R̆ = grw OS,x

is finitely generated, normal, and klt. These are hypotheses here; they are supplied in our
applications by the exact optimizer of Section E. Write S 0 = Spec R̆, with vertex o, and
denote initial symbols by a bar. The fine grading is by the finitely generated value group
Λw ⊂ R, so evaluation at w is injective on this lattice. Its torus will be denoted by Tw .
Taking invariants in each filtered exact sequence gives
                                                G
                                          grw (OS,x ) = R̆G .                                (F.1)

Indeed, the Reynolds operator makes taking invariants exact in characteristic zero. The
weight lattices of R̆ and R̆G have finite index in one another, and hence the same rank.

F.1    Presentations and rational approximations
Lemma F.1 (Weighted presentations). Choose x1 , . . . , xN ∈ mx whose symbols generate R̆,
and put αi = w(xi ) > 0. Any prescribed finite list of functions of positive w-value can be
included in this list. Then

                  O
                  ˆ︁S,x = C[[X1 , . . . , XN ]]/I,        R̆ = C[X1 , . . . , XN ]/ inα I,   (F.2)

where initial forms have least weight. The valuation ideals are exactly the images of the ideals
generated by monomials of weight at least the prescribed threshold. The same description
holds before completion by intersection.
    For every sufficiently nearby linear functional L : Λw ⊗ R −→ R, positive on the listed
weights, the vector αL = (L(αi )) gives the same initial ideal, with its grading composed with L.
Its filtration defines a centred quasi-monomial valuation wL . The functionals can be chosen
rational, and the resulting valuations can be computed monomially on a fixed smooth chart
for w.
Proof. Subduction subtracts a polynomial in the xi with the prescribed initial symbol, then
repeats at a strictly larger value. There are only finitely many values in a bounded interval:
each is a nonnegative integral combination of the finitely many positive αi . The Izumi upper
bound for the fixed valuation w implies that remainders whose w-values tend to infinity tend
to zero in the mx -adic topology. In particular mx ⊂ (x1 , . . . , xN ) + m2x , and Nakayama’s
lemma shows that the xi generate mx . Completed subduction proves both assertions in (F.2)
and the assertion about threshold ideals. Monomial threshold ideals are finitely generated.
Their completed extensions intersect the local ring in the original ideals, by faithful flatness
of completion.

                                                     73
    We record why the dimension and primeness arguments used below are valid also for
other positive weight vectors. For any such vector γ, its monomial filtration is bounded above
and below by fixed linear reindexings of the mx -adic filtration. Consequently its cumulative
quotient lengths grow with exponent n. The associated graded algebra is C[X]/ inγ I;
comparison of its positive weighted filtration with ordinary polynomial degree shows that
its growth exponent is its Krull dimension. Thus this dimension is n. If inγ I is prime,
the filtration is multiplicative and separated, and defines a valuation. If its value group
has rational rank r, the degree-zero homogeneous fraction field is its residue field and has
transcendence degree n − r. This proves Abhyankar equality. Local monomialization of
Abhyankar valuations in characteristic zero [17, Proposition 3.7] then makes the valuation
quasi-monomial.
    Take finitely many series in I whose α-initials generate inα I. Their leading faces persist
after composition with L: ties are preserved because their fine weights are equal, and positivity
leaves only finitely many other terms that could compete in a fixed neighbourhood of α.
Therefore
                                         inα I ⊂ inαL I.
The first ideal is prime, and both quotients have dimension n. A nonzero ideal in a finitely
generated domain lowers its dimension, so this inclusion is equality.
     For the final assertion, use a smooth monomial chart for w at its stratum and expand the
finitely many xi over the residue coefficient field. Their leading faces are finite polynomials in
the stratum parameters. The weights of these parameters belong to Λw . Composing them with
nearby rational L preserves the same leading polynomials. The resulting monomial valuation
dominates the presentation filtration. The induced map from the presentation graded algebra
to the graded ring of this monomial valuation is injective: the polynomial relations among
these leading polynomials are exactly the old relations, unchanged by regrading. Hence the
two valuations agree on every function.

F.2    The canonical calculation before lc equality
Xu–Zhuang study specialization of non-degenerate reflexive differential forms under stable
degeneration of klt singularities [39, Theorem 1.1 and Section 3]. The calculation below tracks
a divisor-free canonical generator, including its finite-group character and any additional
grading, under the finitely generated degeneration used here. We give this calculation before
imposing lc equality because it will also be used to prove that equality after two gradings are
compressed.

Proposition F.2 (Canonical forms under degeneration). Under the hypotheses preceding
Lemma F.1, S 0 has a divisor-free reflexive canonical generator ω 0 . It has the G-character of
ω. It is homogeneous for Tw , and its weight κ satisfies

                                        ⟨w, κ⟩ = AS (w).                                    (F.3)

If an additional torus acts on the original germ, preserves w, and makes ω homogeneous, its
character is preserved separately. None of these assertions assumes AS (w) = w(h). Moreover
the action of G on S 0 is quasi-étale.

Proof. First track discrepancy under the rational approximations of Lemma F.1. On a
fixed monomial chart with stratum parameters z1 , . . . , zr and separating residue coordinates
y1 , . . . , yn−r , write
                               dz1         dzr
                          ω=ψ      ∧ ··· ∧     ∧ dy1 ∧ · · · ∧ dyn−r .
                                z1         zr


                                               74
The quasi-monomial discrepancy formula gives AS (w) = w(ψ) ∈ Λw . Include numerators and
denominators of the rational function ψ among the finite functions whose leading faces are
tracked. The same formula on the same chart then gives the exact identity

                                      AS (wL ) = L(AS (w)).                                  (F.4)

In particular this is an identity of linear weights, rather than only a limiting assertion about
discrepancies.
    Choose rational L and multiply it by a positive integer so that the presentation weights
are positive integers. In this paragraph write v for this integral scaling of wL , and ai = v(xi ).
After shrinking to a G-stable affine neighbourhood, the xi have common zero set {x}. Define
the extended Rees algebra

                       R = O(S)[t, x1 t−a1 , . . . , xN t−aN ] ⊂ O(S)[t, t−1 ].              (F.5)

The threshold-ideal description proves that its special fibre is the graded algebra in (F.2);
positive-level quotients are supported at x, so the affine and local constructions have the
same special fibre. This algebra is of finite type and torsion free over C[t], hence flat. Let
S = Spec R and E = (t = 0). The divisor E is normal, integral, reduced, and Cartier, and
S \ E = S × Gm .
    The total space is normal. Away from E this follows from the normality of S. At points
on E, the nonzerodivisor t and the S2 property of R/(t) imply S2 for R. At the generic point
of E, the quotient by t is a field, so the total local ring is a regular discrete valuation ring.
These observations give R1 as well.
    The order along E restricts to v on C(S) and takes value one on t. Indeed, the residues
of f t−v(f ) are the nonzero graded symbols of f , and the direct grading prevents cancellation
between distinct such leading terms. It is the Gauss extension of v. The monomial log-form
computation gives
                                           dt
                                     (︃       )︃
                                divS ω ∧         = (AS (v) − 1)E.                          (F.6)
                                            t
For example, on a model where the rational valuation v is c ordP , use a uniformizer of P and
separating residue coordinates. The extension has weights (c, 1) on that uniformizer and t;
the wedge of their logarithmic differentials has log order zero, while the remaining coefficient
has value cAS (ordP ) = AS (v). This proves the coefficient in (F.6). There are no other divisor
coefficients, since off E the form is the product of the divisor-free ω and dt/t.
    Since KS is Cartier and v has integral values, AS (v) is an integer. Therefore

                                         dt
                      η = t−AS (v) ω ∧          satisfies     divS (η) = −E.
                                         t
This establishes that the ordinary canonical divisor of the total space is Cartier. At every
codimension-one point of E, the fibre is regular and the flat morphism to the line is smooth.
The residue of η is consequently a rational top form on E with neither zeros nor poles at
any codimension-one point. It trivializes the reflexive canonical sheaf of E. In particular this
establishes ordinary canonical Cartierness, not just Cartierness after adding a boundary.
    Give t weight −1 and xi t−ai weight ai . The residue has weight AS (v) and the same
G-character as ω. If an old torus is present, choose homogeneous generators; t is invariant
for that torus, so its canonical character is unchanged. In the cone case such homogeneous
generators have common zero only at the vertex: their common zero set is invariant and is
isolated there, whereas the closure of every positive-grading orbit contains the vertex.
    Units of a positively graded affine domain with degree-zero part C are constant. Hence
any two divisor-free canonical generators on E differ by a scalar. The full fine grading torus

                                                 75
therefore acts on the generator by a character κ, independent of the rational regrading used
to construct it. For all nearby rational L, its evaluation is L(AS (w)) by (F.4) and the residue
computation. Equality on this open set of rational functionals identifies the two lattice
weights and proves (F.3), including its equivariant version.
    Finally suppose a nonidentity element of G acquired a fixed locus of codimension at most
one on E. At a general point of that locus the fibre, and hence the morphism S → A1 , is
smooth. The fixed locus of the cyclic subgroup generated by that element is smooth there.
Its tangent space is the invariant tangent subspace, and it maps surjectively to the tangent
space of the line: averaging a lift of the base tangent vector gives an invariant lift. Thus this
fixed locus is smooth over the line and has the same fibre codimension on nearby nonzero
fibres. This contradicts quasi-étaleness of the original effective action. The action on S 0 is
therefore quasi-étale.

Corollary F.3 (Lc equality and principal boundaries). If w is h-lc, then (S 0 , div(h̄)) is lc
and ω 0 has the same fine torus weight and G-character as h̄. Consequently ω 0 /h̄ is invariant,
and the quotient cone has the log-form data of Section D. If ∆ is any effective rational sum
of principal divisors near x, with (S, ∆) lc and AS (w) = w(∆), its initial boundary ∆0 is lc
on S 0 . All these assertions also hold with specified additional equivariant gradings.
Proof. Include the finitely many boundary equations in the presentation. Their orders and
discrepancy equality persist exactly under rational regrading. In (F.5), each equation f has
homogenization f t−v(f ) , which is a nonzerodivisor and has no component E; it restricts to f¯
on E. In particular the initial boundary is rationally Cartier. The morphism S → S × A1 is
birational. Comparing the coefficient at its only vertical prime by (F.6) shows that

                        (S, E + ∆S ) −→ (S × A1 , ∆ × A1 + S × {0})

is crepant precisely when AS (v) = v(∆). Its source is therefore lc, and lc adjunction gives
the stated central pair. At codimension-one points of the normal Cartier fibre the morphism
is smooth, so the different is exactly the ordinary restriction of the homogenized boundary.
Apply this to ∆ = H. Formula (F.3) and injectivity of evaluation on Λw identify the weight
of ω 0 with that of h̄. Quasi-étaleness and the finite-map discrepancy formula descend klt, lc,
and the invariant log form to the quotient. A suitable power of ω 0 descends to a divisor-free
pluricanonical form.

F.3    An exact bridge for inequalities
Proposition F.4 (Sign transfer). Suppose w is common h-lc and w(fj ) = 0 for every j. Let
g = U/hb , where 0 ̸= U ∈ OS,x , b ∈ N>0 , and w(g) = 0. Let Q be the actual common slice
and Q0 the common slice defined by h̄ and the f¯j on S 0 . Then

                     q(g) ≤ 0    (q ∈ Q)     ⇐⇒       q 0 (ḡ) ≤ 0   (q 0 ∈ Q0 ).           (F.7)

One may add any finite list of tests of value zero at w. There is no bound on the degree or
the cost of g in this statement.
                                                                                       ∑︁
Proof. Use Lemma E.7 to encode the common slice. Choose positive rational θj with       j θj < 1

The lemma says that Ψ is effective and lc, with exactly the common rays as its nonzero zero-
discrepancy rays. It also gives q(Pj ) = mj q(h) on every common ray. These two properties
let us express a sign inequality as log canonicity of a small boundary perturbation.

                                               76
    Assume the left side of (F.7). For sufficiently small positive rational δ the divisor

                                   Ψδ = Ψ + δ(div(U ) − bH)                                 (F.9)

is effective and lc near x. Here is the exact reason for smallness sufficing. The coefficient of H

discrepancy change is −δq(g) ≥ 0. Checking these finitely many components checks lc on the

not contain x. No uniform choice of δ in U is needed.
